### Predicting Species Splits: A Challenging Fine-Grained Benchmark for Category Discovery
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24672v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24672v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 16:10:02+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 16:10:02+00:00
- **Authors**: Christian Lange, Joakim Bruslund Haurum, Bingchen Zhao, Oisin Mac Aodha
- **Comment**: Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (Evaluations and Datasets Track)
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Widely used category discovery benchmarks have become increasingly saturated and often fail to reflect real-world discovery settings, making long-term evaluation of new methods difficult. We introduce PS-SPLIT, a large-scale fine-grained benchmark for category discovery, motivated by taxonomic splitting: the real-world process by which what was once considered a single species is found to comprise multiple distinct ones. PS-SPLIT contains 79K globally distributed, high-quality, community-collected bird images spanning 771 species, derived from 257 recent real expert-verified taxonomic splits, with associated geographic metadata and full taxonomic hierarchy. The visual differences between categories arising from real taxonomic splits can be very subtle, often requiring significant human expertise to tell apart, making PS-SPLIT a challenging benchmark which we hope will assist in advancing fine-grained category discovery research. Based on taxonomic splitting, we introduce the task of discovery by category splitting, where the goal is to determine which known categories should be subdivided into new finer-grained subcategories. We adapt four generalized category discovery methods to this setting, evaluate existing methods, and show that there is substantial room for future progress.



### DrawScore: Evaluating Generated CAD Models against Engineering Drawings
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24681v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.SE
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24681v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 16:14:30+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 16:14:30+00:00
- **Authors**: Haorui Chen, Shiteng Lu, Zhanyu Guo, Zaixi Li, Yunhe Ni, Yaosong Du, Yichen Xiang, Xiande Huang, Rui Huang
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Automatic CAD generation is commonly evaluated with volumetric IoU, Chamfer Distance, and surface F-score, which barely penalize local errors that occupy only a small fraction of a part's volume or surface area, such as a misplaced hole or a slot on the wrong face, even though such errors are critical to manufacturing. We formalize these metrics in a unified integral form and show that the weight of a local error decays as the $(k+q)$-th power of its size, where $k$ is the dimension of the integration domain and $q$ is determined by the penalty function. Guided by this analysis, we propose DrawScore, which projects both models into three orthographic views with visible and hidden lines, as in engineering drawings, and applies a saturating penalty along the projected curves, so that the weight of a local error decays only linearly. Controlled experiments confirm the predicted decay orders, and on outputs of existing CAD generation agents, DrawScore exposes errors in outputs that IoU rates as nearly perfect. DrawScore further improves generation as a reinforcement learning reward and, for CAD generation from engineering drawings, serves as a test-time verifier without 3D ground truth or an additional reward model. Our code is available at https://github.com/UESTC-Simon/DrawScore.



### Improving Zero-shot 3D Semantic Mapping in Offroad with Geometric Priors
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24696v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24696v1)

- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 16:23:05+00:00
- **Authors**: Eerik Alamikkotervo, Risto Ojala
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Semantic 3D mapping is a powerful representation that enables platform agnostic mobile robot navigation in challenging offroad conditions. Current vision foundation models can accurately predict semantics zero-shot on structured scenes but performance is compromised in natural environments. On the other hand, 3D point clouds encode valuable information for discriminating semantics in scenarios where vision foundation models fail. In this work, we propose fitting a sphericity-elevation distribution for each semantic class to act as geometric anchor. Only the points with the most confident vision foundation model predictions are considered when fitting to produce high quality geometric prior distributions. The geometric prior is fused with the vision foundation model prediction to produce the final semantic mapping. The proposed prior improves SAM3 baseline by +6.3 in mIoU and +8.9 in accuracy on the RELLIS-3D dataset, and by +1.7 in mIoU and +2.1 in accuracy on the WildScenes dataset. The source code will be made public upon paper acceptance.



### Can Omni-modal LLMs Understand Spoken Intentions?
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24706v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24706v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 16:31:52+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 16:31:52+00:00
- **Authors**: Zijie Xin, Tianyi Wang, Daoze Zhang, Jie Yang, Changli Tang, Qijie Wei, Fengyun Rao, Xirong Li
- **Comment**: project page https://xxayt.github.io/O-IN-Bench
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Omni-modal large language models (omni-modal LLMs) can respond to spoken requests directly through voice and video calls. Such requests rely on the scene shared by the user and the assistant, so the same utterance may call for different help or for no response. Most audio-visual benchmarks provide questions as separate text input, and interactive benchmarks mostly score a response as a whole. It thus remains unclear whether a model recognizes when the user is asking for help, and whether its reply meets the user's need once the request is recognized. We propose O-IN-Bench to evaluate how well omni-modal LLMs understand spoken intentions. A model watches a complete video with native speech and outputs one text response, or [SILENCE] when no response is needed. O-IN-Bench contains 1,000 Chinese and English samples in five categories and 16 subcategories. Contrastive pairs combine the same request with different scene conditions to test whether the response changes with the context. A sample-specific rubric judges language, intent, and content separately, and O-IN-Engine verifies each rubric against the video. Across 30 omni-modal model configurations, the best overall score is 59.6, achieved by Gemini3.7-Flash. Gemini3.7-Flash passes 74.4% of the intent judgments but only 42.6% of the content judgments. When the speech is a conversation between peers rather than a request to the assistant, even strong models often keep responding. When a reply must satisfy several people, the best score is only 25.9. These results indicate that recognizing a request and meeting its requirements are separate abilities, and O-IN-Bench measures each of them.



### DRIFT: Detachable Reasoning for Runtime-Flexible and Efficient End-to-End Driving
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24719v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.CL, cs.LG, cs.RO
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24719v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 16:40:13+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 16:40:13+00:00
- **Authors**: Emad Aghajanzadeh, Bethany Anne Wong, Leonhard Kunze, Moritz Thoma, Maximilian Forstenhäusler, Nael Fasfous, Manoj Rohit Vemparala, Pierpaolo Mori, Christos Anagnostopoulos, Daniel Mueller-Gritschneder, Ulf Schlichtmann
- **Comment**: 11 pages, 4 figures, 7 tables. Project page: https://drift-e2e.github.io/
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: The integration of vision-language models (VLMs) into end-to-end (E2E) autonomous driving has demonstrated strong potential by providing scene description and reasoning capabilities. However, VLMs are computationally expensive, and switching language reasoning on and off at runtime without hurting closed-loop performance has not been demonstrated, which hinders practical adoption. To address these issues, we propose DRIFT, an E2E autonomous driving framework that integrates a lightweight language model (LM) for visual question answering and action commentary. By efficiently projecting intermediate driving representations into the language embedding space, we demonstrate that a compact LM (<150M parameters) can achieve strong scene description and action commentary. Furthermore, we introduce a runtime-flexible planning module that is trained on surrogate reasoning features distilled from the LM and can be conditioned on either the LM or the surrogate reasoning features at inference. This design enables bypassing the LM entirely when required by deployment constraints, without sacrificing driving performance. With only 255M parameters, DRIFT reaches up to a Driving Score (DS) of 97.03 and a Success Rate of 89.39% (mean over three runs) on Bench2Drive by interleaving its LM and surrogate reasoning at a chosen reasoning rate. This is on par with the strongest non-language models while being far more compact than existing VLM-based models. DRIFT further achieves competitive results on the real-world NAVSIM and AlpaSim benchmarks at significantly lower computational cost than existing VLM-based approaches. Project page: https://drift-e2e.github.io/



### Multi-Band Galaxy Novel-View Synthesis with Radiative Gaussian Fields
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24723v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24723v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 16:41:49+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 16:41:49+00:00
- **Authors**: Yuzhe Wu, Jialin Zhu, Doğa Yılmaz, Daisuke Kawata, He Wang
- **Comment**: 10 pages, 3 figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: We propose a novel view synthesis tool for galaxies based on Gaussian Splatting (GS). Unlike RGB data, where GS has achieved success, galaxy observations consist of multi-band signals from a massive number of bodies, contain complex patterns of cross-band interactions, and capture light propagation in highly heterogeneous media. As a result, the underlying physical process poses great challenges in model representation and optimization, which have not been investigated in existing research. To this end, we propose a physically inspired Gaussian Splatting method, GalaxyGS, which is equipped with physically meaningful components that capture both the energy emission mechanism of different astronomical bodies and the light propagation between them. In the absence of directly comparable baseline methods, we adopt a wide range of possible alternative approaches. Comparisons conclusively demonstrate that GalaxyGS is better in accuracy and physical fidelity across datasets and metrics.



### Generalization of Text Line Segmentation for HTR in Historical Documents
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24726v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24726v1)

- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 16:43:26+00:00
- **Authors**: Gayan H. Pathirage, Stephan M. Unter, Simon Corbillé, Elisa H. Barney Smith
- **Comment**: This work is going to be published in the proceedings for the Historical Image Processing workshop 2026 (HIP 2026), connected to the International Conference of Document Analysis and Recognition (ICDAR)
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: In this paper we investigate the generalization capability of state-of-the-art object detection models (Hi-SAM, YOLO, and RT-DETR) for the automatic segmentation of text lines in historical documents. Generalization is needed because it is not practical for museums and archives to produce large amounts of labeled samples for each new collection. We also evaluate the few-shot setting by fine-tuning the networks with only a small number of training samples per target dataset. To this end, we use nine different datasets that differ significantly in visual layout and language. Additionally, we discuss why mean average precision (mAP), a classic evaluation metric for object detection, is insufficient for assessing the quality of a text line segmentation model. Depending on the dataset, our CRNN models achieve character error rates (CER) ranging from 2.48% to 7.07% per page for the segmented text lines. This is sufficient for reading comprehension and simple text searches. Finally, we quantify the resource and time requirements of training and inference in different use case settings. This forms the basis for more informed decisions about model and hardware choices based on available resources.



### HandWorldBench: A Unified Benchmark for Hand-Centric World Models
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24731v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24731v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 16:47:46+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 16:47:46+00:00
- **Authors**: Zhaolu Wang, Jiacheng Xu, Lihong Wang, Shuqing Wang, Jianjie Fang, Ziyou Wang, Xvyuan Liu, Rongze Tang, Yiding Ma, Xin Zhang, Jiawei Chen, Wei Wu, Chen Gao, Yong Li, Zhibo Chen
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Recent advances in world models are opening new possibilities for fine-grained embodied tasks, with hand-object interaction offering a particularly demanding setting. Modeling these interactions involves capturing how hand actions change objects and how the resulting states persist over time. Yet systematic evaluation tailored to these hand-centric capabilities remains underdeveloped. To address this gap, we introduce HandWorldBench, a unified benchmark that systematically evaluates hand-centric world models across human hands, simulated dexterous hands, and real dexterous hands along six dimensions: visual quality, hand fidelity, appearance consistency, instruction following, physical consistency, and temporal coherence. To construct the benchmark, we develop a data curation pipeline that extracts hand-object interaction clips and screens them for quality, yielding 20,500 high-quality egocentric clips from over 600 hours of footage. We then manually select 500 clips covering seven task families for evaluation. Benchmarking eleven models under a unified protocol reveals that stronger hand-pose control does not consistently translate into coherent object-state evolution and persistence or robust cross-embodiment performance, with several specialized hand-control models underperforming general-purpose text/image-to-video generators on dexterous-hand tasks.



### Jeveration: System One Models Now Generate
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24738v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24738v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 16:56:59+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 16:56:59+00:00
- **Authors**: Yuhe Liu, Qi Li, Shihua Zhang, Xinchao Wang
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: System One models such as Jev return decisions through fast, parallel prediction. In this paper, we introduce Jeveration, showing that the same decision paradigm can also generate high-quality images in a single forward pass. Jeveration builds on a frozen vision-language model, repurposing its visual-semantic knowledge for holistic image generation. The key challenge is to maintain global consistency. A single prompt admits many valid images, yet parallel predictions must converge on a coherent whole. We therefore condition all positions on a shared random state to coordinate parallel predictions, so that each draw yields a different but coherent image. Since the model tends to ignore this state under direct image supervision, we first learn a distribution over discrete image codes to guide single-pass generation. Beyond text-to-image generation, we further unify image-to-image generation and dense prediction within the same architecture, so that one checkpoint supports all three tasks, each in one forward pass. Experiments show that Jeveration generates a 512x512 image in 78 ms, up to 122x faster than existing unified models, while maintaining competitive image quality and dense prediction performance.



### Density Feedback Control for Transferability of Generative Sparse Adversarial Attacks
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25001v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.LG**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25001v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 16:57:57+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 16:57:57+00:00
- **Authors**: Dongsu Song, Jay Hoon Jung
- **Comment**: preprint
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Generator-based sparse adversarial attacks train encoder--decoder networks to produce transferable sparse perturbations for black-box robustness evaluation. Their perturbation density, however, is set only indirectly by a fixed sparsity coefficient whose mapping to density is unknown and shifts during training, so reaching a target density requires re-tuning; this fixed pressure also yields little transferability gain once density reduction begins. We propose Density Feedback Control (DFC), which tightens or relaxes this coefficient at the end of each epoch according to the gap between the observed hard-mask density and the target, without changing the generator architecture or loss. At a 10\% target density, DFC brings the mean density of three base attacks within one percentage point of the target in a single run and improves the transfer attack success rate (ASR) of two of them on CNN and Transformer targets, including under per-image $\ell_0$ budgets, while perturbing fewer pixels. Tracing GPG's training, we observe that as DFC relaxes the coefficient, the transfer ASR recovers with the density and overtakes the fixed-coefficient baseline with fewer pixels. For EGS-TSSA, which restricts perturbations to source-model feature-importance regions, DFC yields no consistent gain; we analyze how this restriction interacts with density feedback.



### A$^3$Bench: Compositional Reasoning over Aggressive Interactions in Videos
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24762v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24762v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 17:15:58+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 17:15:58+00:00
- **Authors**: Austin C Baker, Raiyaan Abdullah, Daniel Zeev Yoffe, Shruti Vyas, Yogesh S Rawat
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Recent Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have achieved strong performance on general video understanding benchmarks, yet their ability to reason about participant roles in aggressive interactions remains largely unexplored. Existing violence and crime datasets primarily evaluate whether aggression occurs, without testing whether models can identify who performed the action, who received it, and how participants are related within the scene. To address this gap, we introduce A$^3$Bench, a benchmark to study aggressive action and association in videos. The benchmark evaluates whether models can jointly recognize aggressive actions, distinguish aggressors from victims and bystanders, and correctly bind actions to participants under challenging distractor settings. We benchmark 17 multimodal models and find that they struggle substantially on fine-grained aggressive-scene understanding. Performance degrades consistently as reasoning complexity increases, with models frequently confusing aggressors with victims or bystanders, revealing a systematic failure in role-action association rather than coarse aggression recognition alone. We further observe that increasing model scale or generic reasoning models experience limited improvement. Finally, we explore a simple role-graph prompting strategy that encourages structured reasoning over participant interactions, leading to improvements in compositional reasoning. Our findings highlight that reliable aggressive-scene understanding remains a major challenge for current MLLMs and motivate future research on role-aware video reasoning beyond coarse violence detection. Code and dataset: https://github.com/austincbaker/A3Bench.



### 4D-TEMPO: Differentiable 4D-STEM ptychography via fast Physics Optimizers
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24776v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **physics.ins-det**, cond-mat.mtrl-sci, cs.CV, math-ph, physics.comp-ph
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24776v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 17:23:50+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 17:23:50+00:00
- **Authors**: Ramani Duraiswami, Yichao Zhang
- **Comment**: 34 pages: 8-page paper, references, and 23-page supplementary material; 10 figures, 10 tables
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Electron ptychography recovers atomic structure from 4D-STEM data: diffraction patterns at multiple positions of a scanned probe. Current codes reconstruct an image and read atoms off it, or fit atoms under an $L_1$ loss; neither gives per-atom uncertainty. We propose 4D-TEMPO, a differentiable physical renderer that fits the atoms, the microscope, and the detector directly to the recorded electron counts under a Poisson likelihood. The output is a list of atoms, each a Gaussian primitive with a 3D position, a thermal width, and a model-conditional error bar from the Fisher information. The Fisher information also conditions the solver, gives the dose (electrons per Å$^2$) each parameter needs, and separates calibration from imaging. On a simulated WSe$_2$ bilayer, starting from the published reconstruction, fitted atoms cut in-plane error by a third (3.2 vs. 4.8 pm) and depth error by 40-61% against its 3D peaks. Depth comes from multiple scattering into the dark field, the faint counts outside the direct beam. The two layers, $10\times$ closer than the depth of field, separate; per-atom depth then follows the layer geometry. When the model is well specified, the bars are calibrated (69% of position coordinates within $1σ$). On experimental WSe$_2$, fitted atoms predict held-out patterns better than the three published codes we test. With free probe modes where atoms are incomplete, the same likelihood predicts best on all three held-out benchmarks, matches every WSe$_2$ baseline in 5 min against up to 20.5 h, and passes torchslice at its released setting 180$\times$ sooner on PrScO$_3$. Per-atom error bars, planned doses, and fast reconstruction point to low-dose, real-time 3D imaging of beam-sensitive and dynamic materials.



### Counterfactual World Models: JEPAs That Reason About "What If"
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24778v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.LG**, cs.AI, cs.CV, cs.RO, stat.ML
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24778v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 17:24:00+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 17:24:00+00:00
- **Authors**: Randall Balestriero, Yann LeCun, Kerda Varaku
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: World models operate in a world they cannot fully observe. When a hidden disturbance, such as a current pushing a boat, changes the outcome of an action, a counterfactual question follows: what would have happened under a different action, subject to the same disturbance? Its answer separates bad decisions from bad luck. We prove that world models trained on factual transitions cannot answer it on their own, whatever their data or capacity. For many physical systems, the observed outcome and a simple property of the dynamics suffice: natura non facit saltus, nature makes no leaps. When actions and disturbances act as separate forces, their interaction is second-order in time, so the counterfactual outcome is, to first order, the observed outcome plus the difference between the world model's predictions under the alternative and factual actions. This yields a counterfactual world model with no counterfactual data, extra parameters, or retraining. For transitions of duration $h$, we prove an $O(h^2)$ bound on the counterfactual error relative to the disturbance variance, establish a matching minimax lower bound from unpaired factual data, and characterize failures at discontinuities, coordinate folds, and disturbances that scale the command. We validate these predictions from pixels with JEPA world models on frozen DINOv2 and masked autoencoder (MAE) encoders. On four rendered simulators, our approach improves counterfactual $R^2$ from $38$--$46\%$ to $86$--$96\%$ with the same world models; on a fifth, an underwater vehicle, it reaches $90$--$94\%$ with sixteen times more factual data. Four further frozen encoders, DINOv3, SigLIP~2, CLIP and EVA-02, give $81$--$95\%$ on the same simulators. In hindsight planning, it finds the action that would have reached the goal in $35$--$69\%$ of events, against $14$--$23\%$ for an oracle planner that knows the simulator but not the outcome.



### Read Together, Answer Apart: How Earlier Questions Help Vision-Language Models Look
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24785v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24785v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 17:31:56+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 17:31:56+00:00
- **Authors**: Xinyi Guo, Kaile Huang, Xitong Ling, Mingyi He, Chende Zheng, Jianyu Xu, Chao He, Dongqing Song
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Vision-language models (VLMs) are often asked several questions about the same image in a single prompt (co-asking), yet existing evaluations report conflicting accuracy gains and hallucinations at the prompt level without tracking how individual answers change. By pairing each question's single-question and co-asked answers across 22 open-weight VLMs (1B-35B), we find that erratic net shifts reflect two opposing effects: on Densely Captioned Images (DCI), every model repairs 15.4%-29.8% of its single-question errors under co-asking, while the share of correct answers turned wrong falls from 30.8% on weaker models to 4.7% on stronger ones. Across all 14 models supporting custom attention masks, we show through attention readouts and layer-window interventions that earlier same-image questions read visual information into their question tokens in the early-to-middle layers during prefill, helping later questions concentrate image attention onto their queried targets. This visual help accumulates across the scene even across non-overlapping regions, whereas blocking earlier questions from attending to the image lowers both target localization and accuracy in all 14 models. While cross-question help is established during shared question reading, part of the co-asking damage enters later during answer decoding when the decoder attends to other question stems. We therefore introduce Read Together, Answer Apart, a training-free decoding mask that keeps shared prefill intact and isolates each answer from other stems, improving co-asking accuracy on 12 of 14 models on DCI (up to +4.71 percentage points, closing 70% of the pooled gap to asking alone) and on 10, 8, and 5 of the 14 models on WebUI, FinTabNet, and GQA (up to +11.4, +5.3, and +1.1 points).



### Training-free motion statistics match self-supervised behavior encoders
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24792v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24792v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 17:38:27+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 17:38:27+00:00
- **Authors**: Ziwen Chen
- **Comment**: 10 pages, 7 tables, 1 figure. Code will be released with the conference version
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Self-supervised encoders for animal behavior are often trained on, or fed, hand-built movement features. We ask whether they beat those ingredients when compared fairly. A training-free representation, made of generic per-frame measurements summarized over fixed time windows and reduced with PCA, is compared with retrained and released encoders (BAMS, hBehaveMAE) under a preregistered protocol: frozen representations of equal size and one shared classifier. On CalMS21 it reaches 85.8 macro F1 against 82.4 for the best encoder, ties with four labelled videos, and leads when the annotator changes or new behaviors must be learned from a few videos; the reading holds for encoders retrained with three seeds. On the MABe22 mouse triplets it scores 83.7 against 80.3 for the released hBehaveMAE (gap +3.4, 95% interval +2.1 to +4.7). On a second species, the MABe22 fly groups, the same recipe scores 51.6 mean F1 over 50 tasks against 48.2 for BAMS, the best published method. On Shot7M2, the synthetic basketball benchmark built by hBehaveMAE's authors, it scores 68.8 against 64.7 for hBehaveMAE, the best method in that paper, though hBehaveMAE remains better on the shortest movements. Short time windows carry most of the result. Raw keypoints in each body's own frame, summarized over the same windows, already match or beat the encoders on both mouse datasets and on Shot7M2; on the fly groups they fall short, and the measurements add 5.3 points. On these benchmarks, current behavior encoders have yet to beat the hand-built features they learn from, and such baselines should be reported alongside them.



### The Elephant in the Question: Unified Models Picture the Concepts Behind Their Answers
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24793v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.CL, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24793v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 17:38:46+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 17:38:46+00:00
- **Authors**: Tobias Braun, Marcus Rohrbach, Lorenzo Cavallaro
- **Comment**: 32 pages, including supplementary material. Code: https://github.com/multimodal-ai-lab/V-lens
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Visualizing intermediate representations could help us inspect the reasoning steps underlying a model output. Unified multimodal models offer this opportunity by combining understanding and generation in one architecture. Their image generators can access representations formed while processing text. We introduce the V-lens, which uses this connection to visualize a chosen token's hidden state without training a decoder. Across four diffusion-based and two purely autoregressive unified models, the resulting images reveal key intermediate concepts that connect a question to its answer even when the concepts are never named explicitly. Controlled tests show how this content changes with context, token position, and depth. We then introduce lens tuning, which supervises selected hidden states with pictures through the V-lens. Across BAGEL, SenseNova-U1, and HunyuanImage-3.0, the rate of correct visualizations of pronoun referents rises from 2-11% to 86-94%. Surprisingly, such visual supervision also improves text answers without supervising the answer tokens directly. Code is available at https://github.com/multimodal-ai-lab/V-lens.



### Ultimate3D: Teaching Multimodal LLMs 3D Camera-Object Relations by Large-Scale Visual Instruction Data
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24795v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24795v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 17:39:54+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 17:39:54+00:00
- **Authors**: Liu He, Yizhi Song, Ruiqi Xu, Baris Sarper Tezcan, Xiao Zeng, Albert Y. C. Chen, Lu Xia, Shashwat Verma, Sankalp Dayal, Min Sun, Cheng-Hao Kuo, Daniel Aliaga
- **Comment**: Accepted to Machine Vision and Applications 2027
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) struggle with accurately capturing camera-object relations, especially for object orientation, camera viewpoint, and camera shots. This stems from the fact that existing MLLMs are trained on images with limited diverse camera-object relations and corresponding textual descriptions. To address this, we propose a synthetic generation pipeline to create large-scale 3D visual instruction datasets. Our framework takes 3D assets as input and uses rendering and diffusion-based image generation models to create photorealistic images preserving precise camera-object relations. Additionally, large language models (LLMs) are used to generate text prompts for guiding visual instruction tuning and controlling image generation. We create Ultimate3D, a large-scale 3D-grounded 2D visual instruction dataset. Ultimate3D consists of 240K VQAs with precise camera-object annotations, and corresponding benchmark. MLLMs fine-tuned on our proposed dataset outperform commercial models by a large margin, achieving an average accuracy improvement of 29.7% on camera-object relation recognition tasks. Our code, dataset, and benchmark will contribute to broad MLLM applications.



### Edge-Efficient Joint RAW Image Compression and Restoration for Smart Wearable Devices
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24805v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24805v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 17:44:34+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 17:44:34+00:00
- **Authors**: Yunong Sun, Sicheng Chen, Fei Xia
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Smart wearable devices, such as augmented reality glasses, increasingly rely on cameras to understand and interact with their surroundings. However, their compact sensors often capture noisy RAW images, while limited compute and power make high-quality on-device restoration difficult. This creates an emerging need to reduce both local computation and transmission cost without sacrificing image quality. Two common solutions are to offload restoration or to compress the raw data before transmission. The first makes RAW transmission costly; the second reduces the payload but can spend bitrate preserving sensor noise. We address this fundamental mismatch with an asymmetric framework for joint RAW compression and restoration that directly optimizes the transmitted representation for clean image reconstruction. A lightweight on-device encoder compresses noisy RAW measurements into a bitstream, while a higher-capacity backend decoder jointly denoises and demosaics the encoded representation. We use knowledge distillation to train a smaller encoder for tighter compute budgets. Experiments on real and synthetic data show improved rate-restoration performance over strong cascaded compression-restoration pipelines and learned compression baselines. On SIDD, our method improves PSNR by more than 1.5 dB over cascaded baselines at approximately 0.25 bits per pixel. We further validate the method on real 12 MP RAW images captured with commercial smart glasses and measure sender-side latency on both smartphone and smart-glasses hardware. The distilled encoder achieves a 27.6$\times$ NPU inference speedup over on-device NAFNet for 256$\times$256 output patches. The code is available at https://github.com/coolsyn2000/CRAW



### ReCoCo: From Patches to Compositions for Visual Representation Learning
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24806v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24806v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 17:45:04+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 17:45:04+00:00
- **Authors**: Juan Gutiérrez, Saman Zadtootaghaj, José Luis Blanco-Murillo, Sara Atito
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Self-supervised learning has produced powerful visual representations, yet learning strong dense features remains challenging. While consistency between augmented views provides strong image-level supervision, extending this principle to local visual content is less straightforward. Existing approaches often match individual patches across views or identify coherent regions, introducing uncertainties in correspondence estimation and assumptions about how visual content should be grouped. We introduce ReCoCo (Regional Composition Consistency), a principled approach that extends cross-view consistency to the regional level without requiring such correspondences or semantic grouping. ReCoCo represents the content shared between augmented views through compositions of their patch-level predictions over learned visual prototypes. By aligning these compositions, the model learns from regions containing multiple visual concepts without requiring them to be separated beforehand. ReCoCo integrates naturally with teacher-student self-distillation and introduces no additional learned parameters. Extensive experiments demonstrate substantial improvements in frozen-feature segmentation, clustering, and regional retrieval while maintaining competitive image-level recognition, alleviating the trade-off between dense and global representation quality.



### Synthesizing hyperspectral Data using generative Models to train spectral Unmixing Methods for low-cost Crop Residue Cover Mapping
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24827v1
- **DOI**: 10.5194/isprs-archives-XLIX-B3-2026-881-2026
- **Categories**: **cs.LG**, cs.CV, eess.SP
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24827v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 17:58:21+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 17:58:21+00:00
- **Authors**: Ege Artan, Shuo Chen, Andrew Peng, Sammuel Aldrich Karya, Kyaw Thiha
- **Comment**: 8 pages, 3 figures. Published in the XXV ISPRS Congress 2026
- **Journal**: Int. Arch. Photogramm. Remote Sens. Spatial Inf. Sci., XLIX-B3-2026, 881-888 (2026)
- **Summary**: The spectral range of the Field Imaging Nanosatellite for Crop residue Hyperspectral Mapping (FINCH), developed by the University of Toronto Aerospace Team's Space System Division, poses significant challenges for hyperspectral unmixing to determine crop residue cover fractional abundances. The severe lack of standard indices necessitates the use of complex, data-driven unmixing models. Complex unmixing models require dense, well-distributed manifolds, whereas ground-truth datasets are sparse and expensive. To better leverage the information content in existing ground-truth datasets, this study presents a framework that decouples interpolation and abundance-mapping estimation via an intermediary conditional data generator, contrasting with traditional single-model unmixing pipelines. This decoupling enables the inclusion of physical prior knowledge about spectra, which is not natively accessible to unmixing models, thereby artificially expanding the dataset to serve as a Monte Carlo approximation of the population risk. To test this hypothesis, we have proposed two generator models: a 1D Conditional U-Net with Conformer Layers (GD-Streamline) and a Dual-Path Transformer Conditional Variational AutoEncoder (TCVAE), and two unmixing models: Multi-Layered Perceptrons (MLP) and Fourier Neural Operators (FNO). The results of the study indicate a need for rigorous integration and introduction of spectral priors within the scope of the proposed decoupling framework to prevent domain and mode collapse and hallucination by the generator models; otherwise, this leads to falsely approximated data manifolds, ultimately resulting in unsatisfactory and out-of-distribution unmixing performance.



### Does the Action Matter? Probing Action-Conditioned Semantic Forecasting as a World Model for Autonomous Driving
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24840v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI, cs.LG, cs.RO
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24840v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 18:06:20+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 18:06:20+00:00
- **Authors**: Binay Kumar Singh, Niels Da Vitoria Lobo
- **Comment**: 8 pages, 3 figures, 6 tables. Accepted at the WAM-AD Workshop, WACV 2027. Code: https://github.com/binayksingh/does-action-matter
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: World models are increasingly used in autonomous driving to predict how a scene evolves under the ego vehicle's actions, supporting counterfactual reasoning and model-based planning. However, existing evaluations focus on prediction fidelity and planning performance, and rarely verify that the predictions depend on the conditioning action. In this paper, we study action dependence in action-conditioned semantic forecasting, which predicts future semantic segmentation from past segmentations and the ego action. We compare a forecaster with feature-wise and input-level action conditioning against an identical model without action input, and measure action dependence with test-time action interventions scored by standard segmentation metrics. The evaluation is validated in a synthetic environment with known action-dependent dynamics and applied to Cityscapes and BDD100K, both within each dataset and across the two vehicle platforms. Experiments show that the executed action improves mid-term mIoU by at most 2.5 points on Cityscapes and 0.6 points on BDD100K, whereas a wrong action reduces it by up to 23.2 and 7.0 points, below the model without action input. On real data, feature-wise conditioning depends 1.6 times more on the action than input-level conditioning at similar accuracy, whereas both depend on it equally in the synthetic environment, where the history does not determine the action. These results show that an accuracy gain indicates that a driving world model uses its action, but not how strongly its forecasts depend on it. Code is available at https://github.com/binayksingh/does-action-matter.



### Gaspard: Expressive unified speech and gesture synthesis with continuous autoregressive flow-matching and decoupled classifier-free guidance
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24862v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24862v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 18:18:44+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 18:18:44+00:00
- **Authors**: Téo Guichoux, Théodor Lemerle, Catherine Pelachaud, Nicolas Obin
- **Comment**: Preprint - under review
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Human communication involves highly coordinated multimodal signals such as speech and co-speech gestures. Existing approaches for generating both modalities commonly follow a cascaded pipeline, where speech is generated first and subsequently used to drive gesture generation; speech generation is therefore independent of gestures. We introduce Gaspard, a unified speech--gesture generation model based on continuous autoregressive generation with next-step flow-matching predictions, and trained on Seamless Interaction, a large-scale multimodal dataset. The heterogeneous temporal properties of speech and gesture motivate modality-specific prediction heads and decoupled classifier-free guidance over the two generation streams. This guidance rebalances the influence of speech and gesture at inference time, improving expressivity while mitigating dynamics collapse in continuous autoregressive gesture generation. Objective and perceptual evaluations show that Gaspard is a strong gesture generation model against unimodal baselines and that it produces highly synchronous speech and gestures compared to a matched cascaded system.



### NereidVLA: Learning to Understand and Navigate the Underwater World
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24864v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.RO
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24864v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 18:19:00+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 18:19:00+00:00
- **Authors**: Chenjie Zhao, Zhiliang Zhu, Quan Chen, Yuquan Xue, Xingxue Shi, Puyue Wang, Zhiqiang Zhao, Kangye Zheng, Boyang Xu, Mingwei Lin, Dejun Li
- **Comment**: 11 pages, 4 figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Underwater navigation requires translating visual spatial cues into motion, yet marine vision-language datasets rarely include paired robot trajectories. We present NereidVLA, a vision-language-action model that transfers marine spatial knowledge to language-conditioned 3D navigation. We first train Sea2Know on approximately two million marine examples to learn target grounding, spatial relations, and scene structure without paired robot-motion supervision. We then adapt it using expert trajectories executed under simulated underwater vehicle dynamics. Given RGB histories and a language instruction, NereidVLA predicts discrete motion tokens that decode into local 3D trajectories for closed-loop tracking. Evaluation combines an independent six-dimensional underwater navigation understanding benchmark with offline prediction and closed-loop navigation. Under matched navigation training and execution settings, marine spatial pretraining increases simulation success from 72.4% to 84.4%. Without fine-tuning on the real test environment, NereidVLA achieves 78.75% success in laboratory-pool robot trials, compared with 47.50% for InternVLA-N1. These results show that marine spatial supervision can support downstream motion learning and physical underwater navigation.



### Inhabitant: An Agent Framework for Long-Horizon Human-Scene Interaction with Spatial Control and Quantitative Feedback
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25005v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.RO**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25005v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 18:29:20+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 18:29:20+00:00
- **Authors**: Yao Zhang, Zhuchenyang Liu, Yu Xiao
- **Comment**: https://ryenhails.github.io/Inhabitant/
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Recent work on human-scene interaction (HSI) generation moves from single interactions to long-horizon interaction, where a language-model agent plans and executes a sequence of interactions in a 3D scene under one instruction. However, existing agents struggle in challenging everyday scenarios (e.g., picking up a toy and throwing it into a basket) because they lack spatial control over the motion and quantitative feedback on its execution, and they often fail to complete the interaction sequence. To address these gaps, we present Inhabitant, a training-free agent framework in which the agent specifies each motion through positions, paths and poses, measures how the executed interaction deviates from its intent, and revises the control specification accordingly. For an extensive evaluation in such scenarios, we build Inhabit, a benchmark of 75 household tasks in 31 furniture layouts of an apartment, in which every task chains one to three goals on movable objects and is carried out by the whole body, excluding fingers. On Inhabit, Inhabitant completes 98.7\% of the tasks with Claude Opus 5.5, and 93.3\% and 82.7\% with Claude Sonnet 5.5 and Gemini 4 Argon, respectively. Ablations confirm that without spatial control the agent fails most tasks, and without quantitative feedback its motion becomes less precise. Code is available on the project page.



### ShadowTransfer: A Geographic Transfer Benchmark for Overhead Shadow Detection
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24904v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24904v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 18:41:54+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 18:41:54+00:00
- **Authors**: Shagun Mittal, Raymond A. Yeh, Satish Ukkusuri
- **Comment**: Accepted at NeurIPS 2026, Track on Evaluations and Datasets. 53 pages. Project page: https://shagun8.github.io/shadowtransfer/ . Code: https://github.com/shagun8/shadowtransfer . Data: https://huggingface.co/datasets/shagunmittal/ShadowTransfer (DOI 10.57967/hf/11483)
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Overhead aerial imagery feeds dense-prediction pipelines that are routinely trained in one geographic region and deployed in another, yet no benchmark measures how shadow detection models transfer across regions. Existing overhead shadow datasets either lack a geographic transfer protocol or rely on automatically generated labels. We introduce ShadowTransfer, a benchmark of 4,500 human-verified shadow masks across three U.S. cities at two native resolutions, paired with a leave-one-city-out evaluation protocol. Under vanilla training, holding out the test city costs 3.5 to 7.8 mIoU across four architectures, the gap remains significant when the training set grows from 225 to 900 images, and six of eight adaptation methods degrade more than vanilla training on average. We propose a three-probe framework that diagnoses where and how the transfer breaks: the failure operates in feature space, beyond the reach of pixel-histogram matching, shows up as a confidence collapse on shadow pixels while background confidence stays intact, and is recoverable by retraining different architectural components depending on the model: encoder for some, decoder for others. These findings replicate on INRIA building footprints, suggesting they describe geographic transfer beyond shadows. Guided by the diagnosis, we build Spectral Information Bottleneck (SIB), a plug-in module with one component per finding. Across the 9 (architecture, held-out city) cells, SIB has the smallest mean gap to the within-city upper bound of the ten methods we evaluate (6.42 mIoU), level with FDA. The benchmark, LOCO protocol, code, and trained models are released to support reproducible evaluation of geographic transfer in overhead dense prediction.



### PrismRestore: A Compact Restoration Toolbox for Agentic Image Restoration
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24915v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24915v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 18:48:01+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 18:48:01+00:00
- **Authors**: Zhaocheng Yu, Kui Jiang, Jing Cao, Yiang Chen, Xiaocheng Feng, Junjun Jiang, Xianming Liu
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Agentic image restoration composes operations to handle mixed degradations, yet its toolbox often requires storing multiple restoration networks. We propose PrismRestore, which constructs a compact bank of callable restoration actions from a single frozen all-in-one model. Our approach builds on task-dependent yet overlapping channel responses in the shared model. An offline task-prior search uses activation and gradient statistics to group channels with similar response profiles, then searches task-specific modulation coefficients over non-representative channels while leaving representative channels unmodulated. The resulting prior bank enables explicit action selection and composition without additional restoration backbones or task-specific adapter modules. A state-conditioned policy learns to compose these actions using Group Relative Policy Optimization. Its objective separately normalizes fidelity and perceptual feedback within groups of trajectories sampled for the same input, and combines terminal evaluation with return-to-go derived from stepwise quality changes. Experiments on synthetic composite-degradation and real-world multi-task benchmarks show improvements over the evaluated all-in-one baselines and restoration quality competitive with multi-model agents. PrismRestore achieves these results with a single restoration backbone and faster inference than the evaluated agentic baselines.



### Unified Law for Visual Tasks (The Four Laws of Computer Vision)
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24917v2
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24917v2)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 18:48:38+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-19 09:23:07+00:00
- **Authors**: Sen Fang, Yalin Feng, Yancheng Zhang, Yanxin Zhang, Zonglin Lyu, Hongbin Zhong, Liwei Che, Rui Wu, Chen Chen, Dimitris N. Metaxas
- **Comment**: Full version, 227 Pages (Add appendixes, tutorials, and memoirs). This work began in December 2024. Project page at https://world-snapshot.github.io/WSMs . GitHub org at https://github.com/World-Snapshot . This is an empirical article that attempts to unify thousands of visual tasks through a single methodology. Patent pending (inventors: SF and DM)
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: In this paper, we propose a Unified Law for Visual Tasks, which could be further divided into Four Laws of Computer Vision, with the goal of enabling a single framework or method to uniformly train on and perform nearly all visual tasks. Our starting point is an inspiration and hypothesis: if the world information is encoded into image, which we call World Snapshots, then image processing is world processing, and image generation is World Snapshot generation. Furthermore, if all visual and visualization tasks can be reformulated as generation tasks, then a unified method, World Snapshot Models (WSMs), can perform the vast majority of visual tasks, much as large language models have largely unified natural language processing. This paper validates these laws across more than 1,000 trained and evaluated visual tasks (defined by distinct input-output mappings) and presents a unified training framework for future visual-task research and applications.



### $Z$-sight: Unifying Vision-Language-Action and Latent World Modeling
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24940v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.RO**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24940v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 18:59:38+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 18:59:38+00:00
- **Authors**: Junjie Ye, Yiqi Zhao, Muchen Xu, Celina Shiyu Wang, Hao Dong, Ruohai Ge, Rong Xue, Benjamin J Kolligs, Aditya Dutt, Sasanka Nagavalli, Nassim A. Samad, Gaurav S. Sukhatme, Jyotirmoy V. Deshmukh, Yue Wang, Yuliang Guo
- **Comment**: Project page: https://junjieye.com/Z-sight/
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: We introduce $Z$-sight, a unified robot foundation model that couples vision-language-action (VLA) and generative latent world modeling. Video-based world action models (WAMs) show the value of joint future-and-action generation for control, yet inherit the heavy cost of video generators. Recent latent-space policies avoid pixel generation, yet typically align intermediate features or rely on frozen target encoders, decoupling future prediction from the VLM's native feature space. $Z$-sight instead predicts the future directly in the visual token space in which its VLM perceives the present. Coupling a foresight expert and a continuous action expert with the VLM in a mixture-of-transformers, $Z$-sight jointly denoises future spatial tokens and actions at inference without pixel reconstruction or external encoders. Trained on human video and robot trajectories, $Z$-sight achieves 81.9% success on SIMPLE (+9.6 points over the strongest baseline) and 73.3% across six Unitree G1 tasks (+20.0 points) at 110 ms per chunk, while remaining competitive on tabletop benchmarks.



### UltraTex: Aligning Physical Knowledge for High-Fidelity PBR Texture Generation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24941v1
- **DOI**: 10.1145/3842583
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24941v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 18:59:57+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 18:59:57+00:00
- **Authors**: Jingzhi Bao, Xiaohe Ma, Hong Li, Yuanming Hu, Xiaoguang Han
- **Comment**: Accepted to ACM Transactions on Graphics (SIGGRAPH Asia 2026). Project page: https://ultratex.vercel.app/
- **Journal**: ACM Trans. Graph. 45, 6, Article 265 (December 2026)
- **Summary**: Generating physically based rendering (PBR) texture maps for a 3D mesh from a single reference image is highly ambiguous, as the observed appearance entangles material identity, surface reflectance, and illumination. While current generative methods produce realistic albedo maps, they still struggle to infer accurate metallic and roughness attributes for relighting. We present UltraTex, a cascaded multi-view framework that moves beyond appearance matching by integrating physical knowledge into multi-view diffusion-based PBR generation. UltraTex first jointly predicts albedo and world-space shading normals to generate more detailed albedo maps, and then predicts metallic-roughness (MR) maps conditioned on the generated albedo, reference image, and material semantic priors. To inject material semantics, a lightweight adapter transforms hidden-layer features of a vision-language model (VLM) into intrinsic material representation tokens, providing part-level knowledge anchors for MR estimation. To model material-light interaction, photometric representation alignment guides intermediate diffusion features toward ground-truth material renderings under controlled lighting. This makes metallic and roughness variations observable through highlight intensity, specular sharpness, and local reflectance contrast, providing stronger guidance for fine-grained material prediction without explicit lighting supervision. Extensive experiments show that UltraTex generates high-quality textures and outperforms existing open-source and commercial methods in visual fidelity, material accuracy, and physical plausibility.



### Infer3D: Inverse Generative Modeling Enables Robust 3D Reconstruction
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25061v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.LG, cs.RO
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25061v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 19:07:33+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 19:07:33+00:00
- **Authors**: Amani Kiruga, Mark Hamilton, Joshua B. Tenenbaum, William T. Freeman, Vincent Sitzmann, Yilun Du, Ayush Tewari
- **Comment**: 23 pages, 12 figures, 15 tables. Includes references and appendices. Project page: https://amanikiruga.github.io/infer3d-site/
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Humans can understand the 3D structure of scenes even from highly unfamiliar or extreme viewpoints. Cognitive science suggests this generalization stems from a top-down generative inference strategy. In contrast, state-of-the-art 3D reconstruction neural networks typically rely on amortized, feedforward inference. While fast, this pattern-matching approach degrades when faced with out-of-distribution (OOD) scene conditions unseen during training. Inspired by the generative nature of human vision, we propose Infer3D, an inverse generative modeling framework that recovers the latent variables of a generative model to best match the monocular input. To prevent degenerate flat outputs, we couple the generative image prior with a frozen 3D lifting network that constrains the optimization space to a valid, multi-view-consistent 3D manifold. Across a variety of controlled synthetic and real-world tasks, Infer3D demonstrates superior OOD reconstruction accuracy compared to feedforward networks trained on equal or larger datasets. Furthermore, mirroring how human perception requires more time to process confusing stimuli, we introduce an adaptive inference strategy that expends more test-time compute on ambiguous OOD examples while efficiently routing in-distribution inputs. Project website: https://amanikiruga.github.io/infer3d-site/



### VisAlloc: Allocating Visual Supervision for On-Policy Self-Distillation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25069v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25069v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 19:12:00+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 19:12:00+00:00
- **Authors**: Haoran Xu, Tian Chen, Yuhe Wu, Yanbo Zhao, Junchi Liao, Hongyu Wang, Jiaze Li, Wentao Wang, Yanfeng Li, Rui Qian
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) offers a promising approach to improving visual reasoning in multimodal large language models (MLLMs). However, not all samples benefit equally from privileged visual guidance, and uniform token supervision can dilute visually grounded signals. We propose VisAlloc, a dual-level framework combining sample-level visual filtering with token-level counterfactual reweighting. A fixed paired-teacher filter retains samples answered correctly from an evidence-centered positive crop but incorrectly from a spatially displaced negative crop. At the token level, both EMA teachers score the same student-generated response, and their absolute log-probability differences measure view sensitivity. Additional supervision targets tokens admitted by sensitivity or teacher-distribution disagreement, with shared frozen-prior, template-bigram, and student--teacher divergence gates. Inverse-frequency redistribution preserves the total additional weight within each micro-batch, while the positive teacher remains the sole distillation target. Inference requires only the full-image student. Across eight benchmarks, our 4B and 9B models achieve macro-averages of 81.44 and 84.92, outperforming Vision-OPD reproductions by 0.97 and 0.52 points, respectively. Code on GitHub: https://github.com/Kaelyn01/VisAlloc



### MAWAM: A Multi-Agent World-Action Model for Coordinated Manipulation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25105v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.RO**, cs.AI, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25105v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 19:39:30+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 19:39:30+00:00
- **Authors**: Xianzhe Fan, Chi-Pin Huang, Fu-En Yang, Jing Wang, Yecheng Wu, Yu-Cheng Chou, Johan Bjorck, Linxi "Jim" Fan, Jan Kautz, Yu-Chiang Frank Wang, Zhiding Yu, Hengshuang Zhao, Zhiqi Li
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Every move in a robot team changes the world in which its teammates must act. Coordinated manipulation therefore calls for predicting the team's actions together with the physical future they jointly create. We introduce MAWAM, a multi-agent world-action model that jointly generates each robot's future views and executable action chunks in a single sample. Built from a pretrained single-robot world-action backbone, MAWAM jointly denoises all robots' action and future-view tokens through cross-agent attention, while preserving a separate control interface for each robot. MAWAM achieves the highest success on all three real-robot tasks (laundry-bag collection, bath-towel folding, and umbrella handover), with 67.8% mean success, 18.9 percentage points above MA-VLA trained on the same demonstrations and cameras. In simulation, MAWAM reaches 90.3% mean success on eight tasks with teams of up to four arms. Ablations show that cross-agent attention and team-stage future-video supervision both improve coordinated control: without attention, a four-arm relay falls from 95.0% to 39.0%, and without this supervision, Place Food falls from 78.0% to 47.0%.



### Long-Range Video Presentation Attack Detection: A Three-Class Benchmark Across 1-100 m and Two Sensor Types
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25108v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.LG**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25108v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 19:41:53+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 19:41:53+00:00
- **Authors**: Lambert Igene, Mohammad Zahir Uddin Chowdhury, Afzal Hossain, Sandip Purnapatra, Peter Johnson, Stephanie Schuckers
- **Comment**: 10 page, IJCB
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Face presentation attack detection (PAD) systems deployed in border control and airport surveillance are constrained by a critical assumption: subjects are captured at close range, typically under one meter. No existing benchmark systematically addresses multi-distance capture, diverse environmental conditions, and concurrent spoofing and occlusion-based attacks, leaving deployed models underprepared for real-world scenarios. We introduce a video-based PAD dataset of 374,000+ frames from 261 unique identities collected across five standoff distances (1 m-100 m) under indoor and outdoor conditions using a DSLR camera (C) and a mobile sensor (M), with three-class labels: live, spoof, and props (occlusions); models are trained and evaluated on video clips rather than single frames. We fine-tune and benchmark four CNN architectures: MobileNetV3, MobileNet, EfficientNet, and ResNet-50. The higher-resolution DSLR maintains near-perfect spoof classification rate across all distances, while the lower-resolution mobile sensor degrades sharply beyond 50 m, with props classification rate dropping to 55% at 75 m for MobileNetV3. EfficientNet achieves the best overall performance (98.48% accuracy, ACER 2.01%). Generalization tests on unseen attack datasets yield 89-94% accuracy, validating cross-domain robustness. These results establish capture distance and sensor resolution as critical but underexplored axes of PAD difficulty. The dataset will be publicly released under an End-User License Agreement to support reproducible long-range PAD research.



### Do Deep Stereo Models Learn Shape Priors?
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25113v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25113v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 19:51:54+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 19:51:54+00:00
- **Authors**: Linbo Tang, Aneel Damaraju, Todd Zickler
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Localizing object boundaries is important for embodied visual systems, since small boundary errors can change the inferred geometry of physical surfaces. We study whether deep stereo models produce boundaries consistent with simple shape priors when local image and disparity cues are weak. We create a synthetic stereo dataset consisting of objects drawn from a highly constrained family of shapes, with randomized depth, slant, color and texture. Despite the simplicity of this shape space, we find that several state-of-the-art stereo models retain systematic boundary errors, even when finetuned or trained from scratch on the dataset. We then introduce a simple generative stereo model that jointly predicts disparity and a distance field representing object boundaries. The model improves boundary localization and produces contours that adhere more closely to the underlying shape space. These results expose limitations in the boundary predictions of current stereo architectures when local evidence is weak, and motivate further study of whether generative models can better combine regional grouping evidence with global geometric structure.



### TALD: Transferability-Aware Logit Disentanglement for Cross-Modal Knowledge Distillation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25126v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25126v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 20:03:27+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 20:03:27+00:00
- **Authors**: Hui Li, Pengfei Yang, Xinyi Feng, Junhong Liu, Tianyang Zheng, Hui Zeng, Le Dong, Kaibing Zhang, Mengting Zhao, Quan Wang
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Cross-modal knowledge distillation can incur negative transfer when source-modality supervision conflicts with the target modality's discriminative characteristics. We propose TALD, a two-stage framework for Transferability-Aware Logit Disentanglement in class-aligned logit space. Guided by task-dependent self-benefiting and cross-modal exclusivity criteria, a Residual Disentangler decomposes logits from a teacher and an independently trained target-modality reference student into modality-general and modality-specific components. A Sample-Adaptive Modulator regulates decomposition constraints and sample-specific fusion weights. The learned modality-general components are then adaptively fused to supervise the final student. Across five benchmarks spanning vision, audio, and text, TALD achieves the largest mean accuracy gain and highest positive-transfer frequency among the compared methods. Across ten transfer directions with five runs each, TALD improves over training without distillation in 84% of evaluations, compared with at most 70% for the baselines, while offering a favorable accuracy-efficiency trade-off. We plan to release the code.



### GEMS: Generation and Experience Memory for Subgoal-Guided Long-Horizon World Model Planning
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25134v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.RO**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25134v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 20:13:01+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 20:13:01+00:00
- **Authors**: Yangchen Zhao, Ali Payani, Caiwen Ding
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Visual world models enable effective short-horizon goal-conditioned planning, such as reaching nearby targets and pushing objects over short distances, by predicting candidate action outcomes and comparing their representations with a goal representation. However, extending this approach to long-horizon tasks is challenging: prediction errors accumulate over extended rollouts, while latent-space similarity to distant goals provides limited guidance for ranking candidate outcomes. Existing methods address these limitations by introducing intermediate subgoals, either generated by learned models or selected from experience memory. Generated subgoals can contain hallucinations or be inaccurate in aspects such as object positions and timing. Memory-based approaches are constrained by experience coverage and may lack precise guidance for selecting appropriate intermediate states from stored observations. We introduce GEMS, which generates temporally structured subgoals to guide action search and state selection from Latent Experience Memory (LEM). A distance gate determines whether predicted outcomes are evaluated against the generated subgoal or the selected memory state. Experiments across six tasks and task suites spanning manipulation, navigation, and reaching demonstrate the effectiveness of GEMS for long-horizon planning. GEMS outperforms state-of-the-art world-model planning baselines across different manipulation settings, achieving success rates of 92.8% on Cube, 68.4% on Push-T, and 54.1% on Meta-World. It improves over the strongest evaluated baseline on these tasks by 24.0, 7.3, and 22.2 percentage points, respectively. These results demonstrate that generated subgoals and experience memory can jointly strengthen long-horizon planning with a frozen world model.



### CoRelate: Coordinated Human-Object Motion Synthesis through Interaction Relations
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25166v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25166v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 20:46:28+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 20:46:28+00:00
- **Authors**: Zhiwen Qiu, Daisy Lin, Diyu Zhou, Jialin Wu, Rosita Ji, Saleh Kalantari
- **Comment**: 11 pages, 4 figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Human-object motion synthesis requires coordinated human participation as objects move toward specified goals. However, reaching object targets does not ensure that contact is established and maintained, while adjustments that improve contact may interfere with task completion. We introduce CoRelate, a diffusion framework that coordinates full-body human-object motion through explicit interaction relations and task feedback for rigid and articulated objects. We represent intended participation through hand-part relations that specify the participating hand, its part-relative reference position, and participation interval. These relations serve both as generation conditions and as references for measuring interaction realization. At each denoising step, a temporal refiner trained jointly with the generator combines relation deviations, task residuals, and motion features from the current prediction to jointly update human and object motion across the sequence. After generation, temporal motion correction addresses remaining contact failures by optimizing local motion segments while preserving object trajectories and protected human motion. Experiments demonstrate improved object-goal satisfaction, contact F1, and complete interaction success over the evaluated baselines. Ablation studies support the benefits of relation conditioning and the complementary roles of task and relation feedback.



### What a Calibrated Probability Hides: Belief-Level Post-Training for Decision Models
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25168v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25168v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 20:48:01+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 20:48:01+00:00
- **Authors**: Xinye Li, Zirui Song, Huaxing Liu, Xiang Wang, Shuai Li, Zixiang Xu, Yinjun Liu, Zhewen Zhang, Xiaojun Chang, Xiuying Chen
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: A calibrated decision model can predict how often each answer is chosen. The same prediction can reflect disagreement among people who are confident in their choices or uncertainty among people who share a belief. Disagreement calls for identifying whose perspective applies, while uncertainty calls for gathering more evidence. Standard post-training scores a single prediction against one observed choice at a time, learning the pooled distribution without distinguishing these two sources of uncertainty. We introduce Belief-Grouped Policy Optimization (BGPO). The policy proposes a list of beliefs, estimates their prevalence, and predicts a choice distribution for each. During training, BGPO uses Bayes' rule to estimate how likely each belief was to generate an observed choice. These estimates weight the choice's contribution to each belief's log-score reward. To discover rare beliefs, the policy also proposes lists after seeing a few observed choices and retains them only if they predict held-out choices well. At inference, the policy uses only the question and combines the belief-specific distributions by prevalence to produce a calibrated aggregate prediction. On benchmarks covering opinions, conversation safety, emotions elicited by artwork, and photo aesthetics, BGPO achieves the lowest conditional, worst-group, and overall Jensen-Shannon divergence across model initializations. It improves conditional divergence on both text and image tasks. On a synthetic image task with known beliefs, BGPO recovers the underlying groups more accurately than baselines trained with belief labels.



### MV-JEPA: Post-Training Video Encoders for Cross-View Understanding
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25172v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25172v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 20:58:38+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 20:58:38+00:00
- **Authors**: Yangchen Zhao, Ali Payani, Caiwen Ding
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: JEPA-based video encoders learn strong representations for video and image understanding through prediction in latent space. However, their standard pretraining predicts features within individual images or video clips, without explicitly exploiting paired camera views of the same object or moment. Consequently, the learned representations may not reliably support matching across different camera viewpoints. To solve this problem, we introduce multi-view predictive post-training to strengthen cross-view instance and temporal correspondence while retaining independent single-view inference. A shared encoder processes masked context views independently, and a training-time predictor jointly predicts latent tokens for masked context regions and a fully held-out view. We alternate cross-view prediction with single-view masked prediction on general image and video data to help retain the encoder's pretrained capabilities. After only 9,000 post-training updates on a single GPU, MV-JEPA improves cross-view instance retrieval and coarse cross-camera moment retrieval on various datasets, with further gains in zero-shot action-phase retrieval and frozen-feature classification on AE2. Frozen-probe evaluations further indicate that post-training largely preserves motion recognition, image recognition, and dense semantic segmentation performance on the evaluated benchmarks. Together, these results demonstrate that multi-view predictive post-training can strengthen instance and coarse temporal correspondence in JEPA video encoders without modifying the encoder architecture or single-view inference interface.



### Phantom Success, Silent Failure: Auditing the Success Checkers of Simulated Manipulation Benchmarks
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25179v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.RO
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25179v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 21:02:31+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 21:02:31+00:00
- **Authors**: Zirui Song, Jiadong Zhang, Zheng Lu, Xiaosong Ma, Xiaojun Chang, Xiuying Chen
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Simulated manipulation benchmarks measure policy performance using hand-written success checkers that label each episode as a success or failure. Passing an expert demonstration shows that a checker accepts one way of completing the task. Learned policies, however, may stop after partial progress or complete the task in a different way. A checker may then accept an incomplete execution (phantom success) or reject an execution that satisfies the instruction (silent failure). In this paper, we introduce SimAudit, an LLM-based pipeline that audits success checkers against task instructions. SimAudit analyzes the instruction and checker code to identify scenarios where the two may disagree, recreates those scenarios in simulation, and runs the original checker. Multi-view renderings are then used to verify whether the observed behavior satisfies the instruction. Across RoboTwin 2.0, RoboCasa365, and RoboDojo, 183 simulated test cases produced the predicted checker verdict. Visual verification and deduplication yielded 61 distinct defects across 49 tasks, including 40 phantom successes and 21 silent failures. Most defects in RoboCasa365 accepted incomplete executions, most in RoboDojo rejected valid executions, and both error types were common in RoboTwin 2.0. These findings suggest that validating success checkers requires testing both incomplete executions and valid alternatives to expert demonstrations, and motivate more rigorous evaluation protocols for future benchmarks.



### Identity World Model: Recognizing People in an Imagined Latent World
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25204v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25204v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 21:26:47+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 21:26:47+00:00
- **Authors**: Dingqiang Ye, Kartik Narayan, Vishal M. Patel
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Whole-body human recognition has advanced rapidly with large vision models. However, existing methods remain fragile under real-world variations, as they tie identity to transient cues such as clothing, texture, pose, or viewpoint. We argue that a robust recognizer should not merely learn to ignore these cues, but imagine how a person would be represented if they changed. To this end, we propose IDWorld, an identity world model: just as a latent world model predicts the features of a scene after an action, IDWorld predicts how a person would be represented under a changed condition. In a single forward pass, IDWorld imagines these counterfactual features under shared canonical conditions, such as the same outfit, a fixed view, or a bare silhouette, without rendering any pixels. Compared with explicit imagination in pixels, this latent imagination keeps nearly the same accuracy while being over 11x faster, at 5.9 ms per frame. Since each imagined condition has its own strengths, IDWorld then fuses them adaptively, weighting each condition for every probe-gallery pair. Extensive experiments show that IDWorld achieves state-of-the-art results on CCPG and CCGR-MINI, outperforms the compared methods on MEVID, and generalizes best to CASIA-B* and SUSTech1K, with the best mAP on CCVID.
