These results suggest identity world models offer a simple yet effective path to robust recognition. Code will be released.



### Recurrent Vision Language Models Solve the Shell Game
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25210v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25210v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 21:37:20+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 21:37:20+00:00
- **Authors**: Dingqiang Ye, Jiacong Xu, Wen Zhang, Yiqun Mei, Vishal M. Patel
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Tracking a hidden object through a video is effortless for humans, yet it remains a bottleneck for vision language models (VLMs). In the shell game, where a ball is hidden under one of several identical cups that are then swapped, VLMs that answer directly stay near chance, thinking models fail on long games, and fine-tuning learns only the lengths it has seen. We argue that the limit is serial computation: each swap must be composed with the last result, and a VLM of fixed depth has only a fixed number of state transitions for it. To address this, we propose RecurrentVLM, which makes every attention layer of a pretrained VLM recur over time by computing the keys and values of finished frame groups from the layer's own output. With a single answer token, RecurrentVLM reaches 100% on VET-Bench and 99.6% on MET-Bench, and 99% on 50-swap games after training on at most 30 swaps. We also observe several interesting phenomena: length generalization emerges suddenly at an aha moment; without recurrence, each swap costs an attention layer; the ball's state forms in the first four attention layers; and recurrence keeps the latest state at the cost of details of earlier moments. These results suggest that recurrent VLMs are a potential solution for tracking the states of unobserved objects in video. Code will be released.



### Bridging vision-language reasoning and learned control for legged navigation in the wild
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25215v1
- **DOI**: 10.1126/scirobotics.aee1549
- **Categories**: **cs.RO**, cs.AI, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25215v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 21:40:00+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 21:40:00+00:00
- **Authors**: Takahiro Miki, Robin Schmid, Manthan Patel, Turcan Tuna, Julia Richter, Julian Nubert, Joonho Lee, Matthias Müller, Marco Hutter
- **Comment**: 42 pages, 6 figures, 15 supplementary figures. Author's version of the article published in Science Robotics. Project page: https://roam-hiking.github.io
- **Journal**: Science Robotics (2026)
- **Summary**: Autonomous navigation in diverse real-world environments remains one of the greatest challenges in robotics. It demands both robust low-level motion control to handle diverse terrain and high-level reasoning to interpret environmental cues. We present ROAM (Robust Open-world Autonomous Mobility), a system that enables legged robots to perform kilometer-scale navigation in complex alpine and urban settings. ROAM combines the world knowledge of a Vision-Language Model (VLM), pre-trained on internet-scale data, with robust locomotion and local planning policies learned in simulation via reinforcement learning (RL). The VLM provides common-sense reasoning and semantic interpretation of the scene, assessing risks, interpreting signs, and producing semantic labels with associated navigation costs. An open-vocabulary segmentation module converts these labels into a dense, real-time risk map, which conditions two pre-trained RL policies responsible for agile locomotion and local navigation, effectively coupling high-level VLM reasoning with low-level physical control. Real-world experiments showed the system's ability to autonomously navigate a wide range of complex real-world environments. The robot completes kilometer-long hikes with minimal intervention, interprets signs to avoid danger, and evaluates its environment to identify safe areas on which to step. This work represents a step toward the reliable deployment of systems in unstructured environments. However, it also underscores the challenges that remain in VLM reliability and spatial reasoning, indicating that foundational models are a crucial component but not yet a complete solution for navigation in the wild.



### Reject and Report: Forgery-Evident Undetectable Watermarks for Generative Image Models
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25226v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CR**, cs.AI, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25226v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 21:58:41+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 21:58:41+00:00
- **Authors**: Yihan Wang, Haochen Sun, Xi He, Yaoliang Yu, N. Asokan
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Provenance schemes such as those based on content watermarks let images from a generative model be traced back to it. A practical watermarking scheme must be accurate at a low false-positive rate (FPR), robust under content-preserving processing, and undetectable. In addition, it must resist forgery; otherwise, even from a single watermarked image and a surrogate model, an adversary can imprint the watermark onto an arbitrary image or regenerate new content that carries it, falsely attributing harmful content to a model or making authentic photographs appear AI-generated. We argue that a watermarking scheme should not only reject forgeries but also report them: a forgery rejected as unwatermarked leaves no trace of the attempt. State-of-the-art forgery-resilient undetectable watermarking schemes, such as SemBind, fall short on both counts. External anomaly detectors that do report forgeries either miss most of them or falsely flag genuine images after common processing. We propose Tripwire-WM, the first forgery-evident framework for undetectable watermarks. It commits the semantics of the generated image to the magnitudes of the initial latent noise, which the sign-based watermarks leave unused, through a distribution-preserving embedding; a forged image carries a valid watermark but with a commitment that does not match its content, so the detector both rejects it and reports it as forged. Built on the pseudorandom code (PRC) watermark and Gaussian Shading++, Tripwire-WM provably preserves their undetectability. On MS-COCO, with the owner's own model as the surrogate, it accepts no imprinting forgery, compared with up to 55.0% for SemBind, and reports all of them as forged; it also accepts at most 1.8% of reprompting forgeries, against up to 46.2% for SemBind. Meanwhile, it accepts at least 99% of genuine watermarked images at 10^{-6} FPR and matches the robustness of the base schemes.



### MKA: Evaluating Multimodal Knowledge Agents on end-to-end professional tasks
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25228v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.AI**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25228v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 21:59:46+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 21:59:46+00:00
- **Authors**: Anima Singh, Suhas Yogin, Weicheng Kuo, Ivor Rendulic, Ivan Jurin, Antoine Yang, Oran Kelly, Rohan Doshi, Hammad Ayyubi, Ioana Bica, Sergi Caelles, Matthew Conniff, Dan Graur, Anastasija Ilić, Ahmet Iscen, Chao Jia, Tomasz Kępa, Chunyuan Li, Noah Ó Donnaile, Filip Pavetic, Goran Petrovic, Khalid Salama, Zhicheng Wang, Polina Zablotskaia, Yiqian Zhang, Ming Zhao, Benjamin Caine, Mario Lučić, Melvin Johnson, Radu Soricut, Jean-Baptiste Alayrac
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: We introduce the Multimodal Knowledge Agent (MKA) benchmark, which measures how well autonomous agents perform end-to-end professional knowledge work that depends on visual understanding. Each task requires an agent to extract and combine information from multiple visually dense files, such as CAD blueprints, electronics schematics, radiographs, complex charts and geospatial maps, and to produce a standalone deliverable in .xlsx, .pptx, .pdf, .docx, .html or .png format. Existing benchmarks leave this capability largely untested: removing image-viewing tools leaves Gemini 4 Carbon's score on GDPval unchanged, and single-format evaluations such as GDP.pdf and GDP.xlsx do not require reasoning across files or modalities. MKA contains 200 tasks across 10 economic sectors. Deliverables are graded against expert-weighted rubrics by a sandboxed agentic autorater that agrees with human domain experts on 96.2% of criterion judgments in our expert-graded study (Cohen's $κ= 0.91$), scored by weighted satisfaction rate (WSR) overall and on frontier criteria unsaturated by prior-generation models (Climb-WSR). Across the frontier models we evaluate (Gemini 4 Carbon, Claude Fable 5.2, Claude Opus 5.5, and GPT-6 Astra), Claude Fable 5.2 (56.6% Climb-WSR, 73.3% Overall WSR) and Gemini 4 Carbon (54.3% Climb-WSR, 71.9% Overall WSR) achieve the highest scores, while Carbon uses $5.7\times$ fewer tokens. Even for the strongest models, Climb-WSR remains below 57%, leaving substantial headroom for multimodal agents.



### Beyond Restoration: Content-Degradation Factor Unfolding for Real-World Concealed Object Segmentation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25239v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25239v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 22:17:58+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 22:17:58+00:00
- **Authors**: Rihan Zhang, Fengyang Xiao, Yuhan Chen, Sina Farsiu
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Reliable visual perception under degradation requires distinguishing task-relevant content from degradation-induced variations, especially in concealed object segmentation (COS), where foreground cues are inherently weak. The central challenge is not merely improving visibility, but identifying reliable content evidence amid degradation-induced variations. RGB-space segmentation often entangles content and degradation cues, while restoration-based methods primarily focus on image recovery and task interaction. This motivates explicit factorization to distinguish content evidence from degradation effects and assess its reliability. Retinex provides a spatially aligned basis for this separation but remains illumination-centric. We therefore extend it to content-degradation formation with task-asymmetric factor roles. The Factor Unfolding Network (FUN) uses the content factor R as the sole source of direct semantic evidence for segmentation, while the degradation factor L captures degradation-related variations and generates a spatial reliability map to calibrate this evidence. A unified energy couples observation formation with reliability-weighted foreground-background separation, and its alternating updates yield a coarse-to-fine network without clean-image supervision. In this way, FUN factorizes degradation for perception rather than removing it. Extensive experiments across clean and degraded conditions and diverse segmentation tasks demonstrate robust performance and generalization. Code will be released.



### Branch Caching After Step Distillation in Four- and Eight-Step Image Generators
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25248v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25248v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 22:34:48+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 22:34:48+00:00
- **Authors**: Mingyu Liang, Dingkun Xu
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Can a distilled image generator save time by reusing features between its few remaining denoising steps? We study a fixed branch cache that replaces selected attention and MLP evaluations while preserving the student's noise schedule. One full trajectory and one raw-cache trajectory from the same initial noise supply a scalar correction for each branch type and reuse position. At $1024^2$, one internal reuse reduces four-step Qwen-Image-Lightning denoising time from 1.279 to 0.977 seconds. Three reuses yield $1.51\times$ speedup on eight-step Lightning and $1.53\times$ on eight-step Hyper-FLUX. We evaluate image quality on all 200 DrawBench prompts. With the reuse positions held fixed, correction improves mean same-seed image fidelity over raw reuse by 0.66 and 0.87 dB on the two Lightning students. On Hyper-FLUX the PSNR difference is 0.01 dB, while LPIPS improves from 0.1625 to 0.1546. These results measure the effect of branch caching and correction while retaining each student's original sampling grid.



### Keel: A Frontier Model Teaches a Vision-Language Model by Rewriting Its Weights
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25257v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25257v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 22:41:26+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 22:41:26+00:00
- **Authors**: Jiajun Fan, Neil He, Bangji Yang, Qinglong Zheng, Ziwen Wang, Chaoran Cheng, Changyi Yang, Hanqi Mao, Ge Liu
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Can a frontier model repair a vision-language model (VLM) by rewriting its weights? Small reasoning VLMs such as Qwen3.5 need such a repair: in thinking mode they fall into loops on many image questions, repeating themselves until the token limit, spending the whole budget and losing the answer. The usual remedies act on the decoding, leaving the looping model as it was, or must be told what to change. Yet the cause is written in the weights. Keel, the first agentic weight-editing method for VLMs on image tasks, finds and removes it there. Told only the symptom, an editor, a frontier multimodal LLM acting as an agent, locates the cause, edits the weights and tests them, with a failure census and instruments showing it the failing images and where the VLM attends; among several editors, the harness keeps only edits that clearly reduce the failure without lowering accuracy on questions no editor saw. The repair needs no training, keeps the model's size and per-token cost, and applies to already-edited weights. The editors expose where the loops live: from the original weights, independent editors on both models trace them to the attention outputs of the middle layers, and the smaller VLM stops consulting the image inside a loop. On Qwen3.5-0.8B and Qwen3.5-2B, across six image benchmarks from visual mathematics to GUI grounding, the edit removes most loops on every benchmark and shortens answers, and the edited models are significantly more accurate than the thinking models on average; the larger one gains most on GUI grounding, a task absent from the practice questions. Because the repair lives in the weights, it combines with budget forcing where held-out questions favor it: on the best available weights, Keel has the best average accuracy on both models, significantly above every training-free baseline we compare on the larger one.



### Real Cameras are Radiometric
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25265v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25265v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 22:51:43+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 22:51:43+00:00
- **Authors**: Ties Harder, Felix Seegräber, David Nakath, Hendrik Sauer, Kevin Köser, James Pritts
- **Comment**: 22 pages, including supplementary material
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: We propose the radiometric camera, a model that lifts any central geometric camera model to a radiometric one. The lifted model keeps the projection of the geometric model and adds a finite aperture, a free-form focus surface and a generic vignetting function. We calibrate it by inverse rendering directly on the relative irradiance of the sensor, from the same images of standard calibration targets used in geometric calibration. Geometric calibration absorbs the bias of its feature detections. The proposed radiometric calibration uses no features, and our radiometric cameras have lower reprojection errors on held-out test sets. The focus surface extends defocus modeling from the thin lens to any central camera, and we use it in a first multi-view radiometric reconstruction, which recovers the in-focus reflectance of a texture from defocused images. The vignetting is estimated jointly and accurately reproduces a flat field measured through a diffuser. Because every standard camera model lifts to a radiometric one, the radiometric camera naturally and directly extends generative scene models beyond multi-view geometry to multi-view radiometric ones, where phenomena such as lighting and attenuation can be accurately estimated.



### Accord: Universal Online Metric Localization and Dense Mapping from one Camera and an IMU
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25279v1
- **DOI**: None

- **Authors**: Jaafar Mahmoud, Arthur Movsesyan, Cuong Huynh, Mikhail Iumanov, Sergey Zagoruyko, Sergey Kolyubin
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Dense metric mapping from a single camera and IMU remains challenging: visual-inertial odometry (VIO) provides metric motion but sparse geometry, whereas feed-forward depth models predict dense structure without ensuring metric scale. We present Accord, which constructs an accurate online dense metric map from any camera with an IMU. A VIO filter publishes metric poses, a depth foundation model conditioned on them predicts depth for short keyframe windows, and a factor graph fuses the resulting submaps. Recent work has explored several approaches to this problem. What is essential is the multi-view agreement (Accord), which is considered in SLAM as a source of geometric self-consistency. Accord anchors each keyframe window to metric 3D points triangulated from the filter poses, then exploits bidirectional dense depth agreement between co-visible images to estimate and correct per-image scale errors while preserving the metric scale established by the anchors. Context keyframes further improve depth prediction, and the resulting submaps are fused in a factor graph. We evaluate a single mapping configuration across five public camera-IMU setups and a new Phone-rig/GoPro dataset. With nothing tuned per dataset/setup and every map aligned only by Accord's own trajectory, Accord achieves consistently best/second-best mean F-score at 5 cm on every platform. Accord is open-source along with the phone-rig dataset for the benefit of the research community



### Persistent Identity in Person Tracking: Online Enrollment and Registered Recognition
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25283v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25283v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 23:20:51+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 23:20:51+00:00
- **Authors**: Ratha Siv, Matei Mancas, Bernard Gosselin
- **Comment**: 7 pages, 2 figures, 7 tables. An earlier version of this paper was submitted to an IEEE conference
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: We extend PoseTReID with online appearance-bank matching (Gallery) and a residual-conflict guard for registered SVM recognition. All methods use supplied ground-truth (GT) person boxes. We average all-box assessment IDF1/HOTA equally across videos. On six SportsMOT clips, StrongSORT leads standalone trackers (79.44/84.72%) and exceeds both Gallery variants. Over eleven videos, Gallery AIN (83.29/85.44%) and COPE (84.66/86.38%) exceed standalone trackers with complete results. This lead depends on two retrospectively added GTA development compilations and is absent on the original nine videos. Registered SVMs lead both cohorts among complete results, with AIN at 95.72/94.82% over eleven videos. They use every GT crop from approximately 20% of frames, including future appearances. On MOT20-03, registered AIN reached 96.80% IDF1 and assigned the correct enrolled name to 90.72% of assessment GT observations. Gallery and standalone trackers receive no per-video labelled enrollment. Both identity routes restore identities across internal title cards, but Gallery trails SORT on three videos (four in HOTA) and both SVMs trail SORT on MOT16-04/MOT20-03. Under six-clip persistent-only scoring, native Gallery gains 2.39 percentage points in mean IDF1 over a matched control, with lower correct-P coverage and 5.02 times as many encoder attempts. Pose gating is mixed; the guard's metric effect is not isolated.



### CalibCache: Cache-to-Full Feature Dynamics Calibration for Diffusion Transformers
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25296v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25296v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 23:43:52+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 23:43:52+00:00
- **Authors**: Mingyu Liang, Dingkun Xu, Jingwei Xu
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Diffusion Transformers generate high-quality images, but iterative sampling remains computationally expensive. Feature caching reduces the number of full network evaluations, yet its approximation error can become more severe under short sampling schedules, where fewer full-compute steps and a coarser sampling grid can make history-based extrapolation less reliable. In this paper, we propose CalibCache, a lightweight offline calibration method that corrects systematic discrepancies between cached and full-compute feature dynamics. Specifically, CalibCache calibrates feature acceleration, defined as the second-order finite difference across sampling steps. Using only a single pair of full-compute and naive-cache trajectories at each setting, CalibCache fits scalar acceleration maps offline by centered least squares. These maps are then applied during sampling without additional full-network evaluations or online optimization. Across FLUX.1-dev and Qwen-Image on DrawBench and DiT-XL/2 on ImageNet-256, CalibCache consistently improves fidelity or generation quality over TaylorSeer under 20-step sampling at cache intervals $N=3,4,5$. At $N=5$, it reduces FLUX.1-dev LPIPS from $0.563$ to $0.429$ while achieving a $2.86\times$ denoising speedup over full-compute sampling, and lowers DiT-XL/2 FID from $16.39$ to $10.37$ relative to TaylorSeer.



### ScaleDiag: Diagnosing the Limits of Test-Time Scaling in GUI Grounding
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25297v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25297v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-18 23:49:44+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 23:49:44+00:00
- **Authors**: Chen Liu, Zhen Fang, Xin Yu, Dadong Wang, Ling Chen
- **Comment**: Accepted by ICDM@D2AI 2026 Best Paper Award
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: GUI grounding maps a screenshot and a natural language instruction to the coordinates of the intended interface element. Existing approaches improve grounding through visual scaling, instruction scaling, and sampling scaling, yet it remains unclear which grounding bottlenecks these strategies address and where their gains saturate. To address this gap, we introduce ScaleDiag, a controlled diagnostic framework that isolates the effects of visual input, instruction information, and prediction sampling on GUI grounding by independently varying target scale and surrounding context, target-specific information and auxiliary descriptions, and candidate coverage and selection, respectively. We evaluate ScaleDiag across five benchmarks and up to eight vision-language models and find that each scaling strategy operates within a much narrower regime than commonly assumed. (1) Visual scaling is governed by target occupancy rather than a fixed crop ratio, with full-screen errors typically rescued once the target occupies only 0.32% of the cropped view, while further zooming eventually becomes harmful. (2) Instruction scaling is driven by information that identifies the target rather than description richness itself, as even ground-truth-derived attributes such as color, size, and shape are often neutral or harmful, while appearance and spatial descriptions account for over 90% of the achievable gain. (3) Repeated sampling exposes a different limitation, as correct coordinates are frequently generated but remain difficult to select, leaving 5.1--11.0 points of oracle headroom while geometric consensus provides almost no improvement. Together, these results show that test-time scaling is constrained by distinct operating regimes rather than benefiting monotonically from more visual focus, richer instructions, or additional samples. Our code is available at https://github.com/YenanLiu/ScaleDiag.
